### Page 5

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 16 October 2026

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  What are your key takeaways from US bank earnings? 
Our view: Q3 2026 earnings results from major US banks have 
been mixed, but underlying trends remain encouraging. Healthy 
loan and deposit growth, resilient consumer spending and 
wealth management activity continue to support the sector. 
Credit risks remain manageable. We remain constructive on the 
US financial sector, favouring stronger, diversified franchises. 
Rationale: Healthy loan and deposit growth continue to support net 
interest income (NII), hinting that the US economy remains resilient. 
Capital markets revenue grew year -on-year (y/y) , but moderated 
sequentially after a very strong Q2 2026, although performance 
varied across banks. Management commentary points to a healthy 
deal pipeline , supported by M&A activity and debt refinancing 
demand, although execution will depend on market conditions. 
Provisioning and net charge -off trends were mixed across banks, 
with no clear signs of broad deterioration in credit quality. Firmer 
US inflation raises the risk of further monetary tightening. While 
higher rates can support asset yields, they can also increase 
funding costs, pressure borrowers and weigh on securities ’ 
valuations. We will continue to monitor lending demand, credit 
costs, NII trends, capital markets momentum and capital returns.  
—  Robin Xie, Senior Investment Strategist 
  Can Big Tech’s earnings guidance sustain confidence in 
the AI investment cycle? 
Our view: We expect Big Tech’s AI spending to remain elevated, 
supporting our constructive AI capex outlook. However, we 
await guidance on the pace of capex growth and the 
sustainability of profit margins. 
Rationale: Semiconductor leaders’ latest company guidance points 
to supply constraints extending through 2028, supporting continued 
capacity expansion for compute . However, while AI capex is still 
likely to rise, y/y growth is expected to moderate  from c.100% in 
Q3 2026 to c.20% in Q3 2027. This normalisation in growth could 
raise concerns about the AI investment cycle’s durability.  
The Nasdaq-100 Index has rallied alongside accelerating AI capex. 
Slower capex growth is likely to moderate semiconductor revenue 
growth, while hyperscalers continue to incur depreciation costs on 
past investments. The slower growth is already partially reflected in 
the Nasdaq -100’s valuation de -rating this year. While we remain 
constructive on the AI investment theme, we prefer to stay 
prudent in the near term and await Big Tech’s guidance for greater 
visibility into spending plans in H2 2027 and beyond. 
—  Ryan Goh, Investment Strategist

US banks’ revenues remain resilient despite

### Page 6

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 16 October 2026

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  Would France face a credit rating downgrade by rating 
agencies this month? What might be the market implications? 
Our view: A rating downgrade is unlikely unless France’s 2027 
budget passes the National Assembly very soon with material 
changes to the proposal. We see French government bond 
yields remaining elevated relative to German Bund yields. 
Rationale: The French National Assembly began debating France’s 
2027 draft budget on 13 October 2026, with the first vote scheduled 
for 20 October. Near-term budget approval appears unlikely, as 
opposition parties have tabled motions to reject the proposal. The 
French Parliament has up to 70 days from the budget’s submission 
to complete the legislative process, although  its final adoption and 
the resolution of political challenges could extend into early 2027.  
Last October, two of the three major rating agencies downgraded 
France by one notch to the high-single-A category  with stable 
outlooks. The remaining agenc y rated France in the low-AA 
category with a negative outlook. As negative outlooks are typically 
resolved within 12 -18 months, agenc ies have time to await fiscal 
clarity before acting. We believe downgrade risks are largely priced 
in after the recent rise in yields, but volatility is likely to persist amid 
fiscal uncertainty and the approaching presidential election. Banks 
and domestically-focused issuers would face the greatest near-term 
pressure. However, we would view wider spreads as an opportunity 
to add exposure, particularly to French banks’ subordinated 
debt, given their stronger balance sheets and the fact that France 
contributes less than 50% of major French banks’ overall revenue. 
—  Ray Heung, Senior Investment Strategist 
  Are you still positive on Japanese banks? 
Our view: We are positive on Japanese banks despite the recent 
market correction, as the structural drivers of improving 
profitability and shareholder returns remain intact . While 
political risk and shifting BoJ rate hike odds could sustain near-
term volatility, we see the recent pullback as an opportunity to 
build selective exposure to Japanese bank stocks. 
Rationale: Japan’s gradual monetary policy normalisation  
should support banks’ NII as floating-rate loans reprice and maturing 
assets are reinvested at higher yields. Healthy corporate lending 
demand, improving capital efficiency and continued share buybacks 
provide further support . While uncertainty over the pace of BoJ 
tightening could weigh on sentiment, we see gradual interest rate 
normalisation as supportive of medium-term bank profitability.  
Key risks  include rising deposit costs, mark -to-market losses on 
bond portfolios and a disorderly increase in Japanese government 
bond yields. We continue to favour well-capitalised banks  with 
strong domestic deposit franchises and improving returns on equity. 
—  Robin Xie, Senior Investment Strategist

### Page 7

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 16 October 2026

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  Could NZD/USD rebound from its one-year trough? If so, 
how far could it recover?  
Our view: New Zealand’s improving domestic conditions, 
persistent inflation and technical support provide a basis for 
NZD/USD’s initial recovery towards the 0.57 level, although 
elevated US bond yields could slow its progress. 
Rationale: In Q3, New Zealand’s  seasonally-adjusted business 
confidence reading rose to a net 40% of firms expecting the economy 
to improve, up from net 14% in Q2 . Hiring intentions improved, and 
filled jobs rose 0.2% m/m in August. These developments suggest 
domestic conditions remain supportive for the NZD.  
New Zealand’s above-target inflation keeps further RBNZ tightening 
in focus. The 22 October consumer inflation data release and the 
28 October RBNZ policy meeting are key catalysts: persistent price 
pressures a nd improving demand are expected to prompt firmer 
policy guidance and strengthen the NZD’s interest-rate appeal. 
NZD/USD’s position near the lower boundary of its downtrend 
channel suggests scope for a technical rebound. We see 0.57 as 
the first recovery hurdle; a sustained break  above this level  would 
strengthen the case for further gains. Firmer US  consumer inflation 
remains a headwind, making a gradual recovery more plausible 
than a sharp reversal.  
—  Vincent Tan, Senior Investment Strategist 
—  Iris Yuen, Investment Strategist 
  Can the proposed Russia -Ukraine ‘energy ceasefire ’ 
contain oil prices as Middle East risks rise? 
Our view: We expect escalating Middle East tensions to keep oil 
prices elevated . We  maintain our  three- and 12 -month West 
Texas Intermediate (WTI) oil price forecasts at USD 100/bbl and 
USD 80/bbl, respectively, and continue to favour US Treasury 
Inflation-Protected Securities (TIPS) as a portfolio hedge 
against persistent inflation risks. 
Rationale: The proposed Russia-Ukraine ‘energy ceasefire’ still 
looks some way off. In our view, even if an agreement is reached, it 
would do little to ease the supply risks building in the Middle East. 
Gulf oil exports have recovered to above 80% of pre-conflict levels 
in recent weeks, helping moderate the pace of price increases. 
However, the recovery remains vulnerable to disruptions, particularly 
following the latest Houthi attacks on Saudi energy infrastructure . 
Diesel and jet fuel remain under the most pressure , with Gulf 
exports of these fuels in September  at only around 60% of pre -
conflict levels. A sustained disruption to major Saudi production or 
export infrastructure remains the principal upside risk to oil prices. 
—  Anthony Naab, Investment Strategist

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
3
OVERVIEW
Coming into the home strait for 2026,
the Australian consumer has again
got the staggers. Spending has held
up surprisingly well given the intense
headwinds that have buffeted household
budgets over the year. But the same cannot
be said for sentiment which has fallen off
badly in the face of more shocks.

Our October Red Book surveys the scene.
Psychologically, consumers has seen a
return to the ‘cost of living nightmare’
they struggled with through much of
2022, 2023 and 2024. Global forces
and domestic factors are again having
pervasive negative impacts on budgets.
The scale may not be as severe as the
previous episode – inflation is running in
the 3-4% range not the 7-8% and interest
rates have risen 1ppt not 4 – but the
feeling is similar. In part this because there

has was very little reprieve in between –
despite tax cuts and a partial unwind of the
interest rate tightening in 2025, household
incomes never got a chance to regain the
lost ground.
The inflation ‘relapse’, and the difficulty
the RBA and governments seem to be
having in managing the situation, is likely
feeding into fears about the outlook for the
wider economy as well.
Exactly what this means for spending is
not entirely clear. As discussed in our last

report , sentiment reads have been much
weaker than actual spending in recent
years. The apparent disconnect is likely
due to a mix of legacy effects from COVID,
and the specific challenges posed by high
inflation (including the lingering effects
of high prices even once inflation has
subsided).
On balance we expect the spending side
to slow materially, particularly as we
head into year end. Indeed, the seven
weeks between now and Christmas
could prove decisive for the consumer

outlook, beginning with another important
sentiment survey update and then the high
season for retail. How spending performs
outside of key sales windows will be most
telling.
Staggering into year end

1. Consumer spending: slowdown ahead
“... sentiment ... has fallen off badly in the face of

### Page 4

or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
4
The Westpac–Melbourne Institute
Consumer Sentiment Index declined 4.2%
over the three months to Oct, unwinding a
mini-rally through the middle of the year.
At 80.4 the average read over the Oct
survey implies that pessimists outnumber
optimists by nearly 20%, a deeply
pessimistic level by historical standards

that is amongst the forty worst reads since
the survey began in the 1973.
The survey detail shows concerns are still
primarily about the cost-of-living and high
interest rates than the more acute job and
insolvency problems that arise during a
recession. The RBA’s Sep rate hike, mid-
way through the survey week looks to have
badly rattled consumers.
The component detail shows a broad
pull-back but with bigger declines in
assessments of family finances and

buyer sentiment and milder declines in
assessments of the economic outlook.
Consumers remain intensely risk averse.
The Westpac Risk Aversion Index held near
historical highs in Sep, readings still only a
touch of the 52-year high of 60.8 recorded
in June 2023. Recall that the index is based
on responses to questions on the ‘wisest
place for savings’. Responses show ‘safe
options’ remain very heavily favoured.
The sentiment mix points to a significant

weakening in spending momentum is
weakening. CSI±, a modified indicator
that shows a better correlation with per
capita spending, points to a shift back to
significant outright contractions in per
capita spending. That said, spending
in 2026 has held up better than these
indicators have suggested so far.
Consumers are on high alert for interest
rate. The Westpac–Melbourne Institute
Mortgage Rate Expectations Index rose
5.5% to 179 over the three months to

Oct, just shy of May’s cycle peak of 181.
The RBA has raised interest rates 25bps
since then. The Nov survey update due Nov
10 will be an important update on how
expectations have evolved.
The ‘time to buy a major item’ index
dropped 4.3% over the last three months,
taking it back near the lows seen earlier
in the year and a full 40pts below the
long run avg. Durables spend has been
mixed over the first half of 2026, posting a
surprisingly solid gain in Q2 but with most

of this centred on new vehicle spend where
‘extenuating circumstances’ were at play.
Homebuyer sentiment has been
understandably unsettled in recent months
given heightened uncertainty around
interest rate and tax policy changes amid
evidence of a broadening downturn in
established markets.
The ‘time to buy a dwelling’ index actually
lifted 3.6% over the three months to Oct
but is still at firmly pessimistic levels. Both
monthly reads and sub-group detail have

been very unsettled.
The broadening and deepening downturn
in Australia’s housing markets is seeing
an equally rapid shift in consumer house
price expectations although these have not
turned as negative as they did during the
2022-23 correction.
The Westpac–Melbourne Institute
Consumer House Price Expectations Index
declined a further 2.4% over the 3mths to
Oct, following a steep 25% drop over the
previous 3mths. At 115.6, the Oct index
read is miles below the 15yr high of 174 at

the start of the year and below the long run
avg of 130. That said, the read is still above
100, meaning more consumers expect
prices to rise than fall.
The labour market remains an island of
relative calm for consumers. The Westpac–
Melbourne Institute Unemployment
Expectations Index rose 9.3% over the
three months to Oct, reversing most of
the 12.1% improvement over the previous
three months (recall that higher reads mean
more consumers expect unemployment to

rise over the year ahead). At 142.2, the Oct
index read is now decisively above the long
run avg of 129 again but  still well below
the levels that would sound more worrying
alarms.
As noted, the Nov survey, currently in the
field and due to be released on Nov 10,
will provide critical updates on many of
these themes.
Quick run-down

### Page 5

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
5
THE CONSUMER MOOD
• Consumers remain stuck in a cost-of-
living nightmare that seems to have
not end in sight. Sentiment remains
deeply pessimistic, weighed down by
continued uncertainty about the path
for inflation and interest rates, the
latter providing a clear negative ‘jolt’
midway through the Oct survey week.

• The Westpac Melbourne Institute
Index of Consumer Sentiment declined
4.2% over the three months to Oct,
unwinding a mini-rally through the
middle of the year. At 80.4 the average
read over the Oct survey implies that
pessimists outnumber optimists by
nearly 20%, a deeply pessimistic level
by historical standards. It is amongst
the forty worst reads since the survey
began in the 1973. We have had
two others in this unfortunate group
already this year (in Apr and Jun) with

another eight in 2022–23. Altogether
this marks the worst period of recurring
extremely weak sentiment since the
disastrous recession in the early 1990s.
• The survey detail shows consumer
concerns are still primarily about the
cost-of-living and high interest rates
than the more acute job and insolvency
problems that arise during a recession.
• Average weekly pump prices pushed
back over $2.30/litre nationally in early
Oct, rising to over $2.35/litre since. This

is near the peaks seen back in April and
up nearly 25% since the start of the
year. Meanwhile the RBA interest rate
rise in late Sep took the cash rate to
4.6%, the highest since 2011, with the
standard variable mortgage interest
rate pushing above 9% for the first time
since 2008.
• The RBA’s Sep move badly rattled
consumers. Sentiment amongst the
60% of the sample surveyed prior came
in at 86.9, up slightly from the Sep
read. The sentiment read across the

40% surveyed after the RBA decision
came in at just 67.2, an alarmingly weak
read that, for complete surveys, has
only been registered during the depths
of the early 1990s recession. The nearly
20% drop between pre and post RBA
samples is also the largest negative
swing we have seen since we started
tracking daily responses in 2019.
• The RBA has delivered another hike as
we go to press, meaning Nov is likely
to see yet another interest rate hike

to sentiment (the survey result will be
released on Nov 10).

3. Consumer sentiment: finances, economic conditions

### Page 6

or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
6
THE CONSUMER MOOD
• The component detail shows a broad
pull-back but with bigger declines in
assessments of family finances and
buyer sentiment. The ‘finances vs a
year ago’ sub-index dropped 6% over
the 3mths to Oct  to just 66.9, on a par
with April’s extreme low. The pressure
is not expected to ease up any time

soon, the ‘finances, next 12 months’
sub-index also down 8.3% to 88.4, in
the bottom 10% of reads for this sub-
index since the survey began.
• Consumer views on the economy
recorded milder declines, the ‘economy,
next 12 months’ sub-index unchanged
on Jul, holding at 78.3 and the
‘economy, next 5 years’ sub-index down
1.8% to 85.5. However, the latter is now
back near the lows seen in Aug 2020.
This was a dark time during the COVID
crisis when Melbourne had entered

its second lockdown and just prior to
the Federal government significantly
expanding its JobKeeper supports. The
readings suggest there are shades of
COVID now with a similar sense of
helplessness – that Australia is unable
to avoid a global inflation problem and
that there is relatively little authorities
can do to help the situation that does
not add to pressures on consumers.
• The cost of living and interest rate
pressures are both broad and intense.

Pessimists outnumber optimists in 102
of the 106 sub-groups we track, and by
over 20% in nearly two thirds of these.
This speaks to the pervasive influence
of higher fuel costs in particular.
• That said, sentiment is becoming more
dispersed. It is possible to compute
sentiment index scores for each
individual respondent. Chart 4 shows
how the spread across these scores
has evolved since 1995. The average
standard deviation over the full history
is 46pts. It was about 4pts lower over

the decade prior to COVID but has risen
steadily since to over 52pts in Oct, the
average spread over the last 12mths
marking a record high. Chart 5 shows
the full distribution of individual scores
in the last 3mths compared to history.
• We noted in our last report that the
widening spread has been accompanied
by a notable divergence in sentiment
across major and minor party voters.
There are likely multiple underlying
reasons for the shift which the voter

group split may be picking up indirectly
rather than directly due to the way these
skew across different demographics.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
7
Spending: squeeze ahead
• Our CSI± composite combines sub-
indexes tracking views on ‘family
finances’ and ‘time to buy a major
item’ with the Westpac Consumer Risk
Aversion Index and usually provides a
good guide to trends in spending over
the next 3–6mths.
• However, as discussed in detail in our
last report the composite has been of

more limited use in recent years due
to both the volatile spending around
COVID lock-downs and the nature of
the post-COVID cost-of-living shock
to sentiment. Analysis shows adding
the WestpacMelbourne Institute Index
of Unemployment Expectations (p15)
to the composite goes some way to
redressing these issues. Accordingly,
our CSI± indicator will now include this
additional component going forward.
As Chart 6 highlights, the composite
should still mainly be viewed as a

directional guide to where per capita
spending growth momentum is heading
rather than a point estimate.
•  Unsurprisingly, the CSI± indicator fell
steeply over the 3mths to Oct, dropping
from 75.6 to 67.6, putting the measure
over 26pts below its long run avg of
93.8.
• The move reflected the more marked
falls in assessments of finances
and ‘time to buy’ with a significant
deterioration in unemployment
expectations as well. Risk aversion
remained unchanged at very high levels.

• Chart 6 shows the current read is
consistent with a large contraction in
real per capita consumer spending,
even allowing for the wedge that has
opened up between observed spending
growth and the CSI± indicator over the
last few years.
• The Q2 national accounts showed some
slowing in consumer spending although
it was more resilient than feared to the
combined effect of higher fuel prices
and interest rates while incomes posted
a robust rise. More generally a range

of shocks and shifts made for a messy
quarter (see here for more).
• Real consumption rose 0.4%qtr taking
annual growth to 1.8%yr, down from
a peak of 2.7%yr in Q3 last year. The
detail showed declines in fuel spend
and transport services (including air
travel) were more than offset by a 10%
surge in vehicle sales, led by a big rise
in EVs and hybrids.

SENTIMENT INDICATORS

### Page 8

or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
8
• Household disposable incomes rose
0.6%qtr, lifting annual growth to
2.3%yr, supported by a continued
robust rise in labour income (more due
to average compensation than head
count in the latest quarter). This in
turn meant the household savings rate
ticked up slightly from 6.4% to 6.5%.
• New annual benchmarks released by

the ABS since the Q2 accounts were
published showed relatively minor
revisions (Chart 7). For the full financial
year, annual growth in disposable
income was nudged 0.2pts lower to
2.1%yr with real spending shaved back
from 1.8%yr to 1.7%yr and the savings
rate lowered from 6.5% to 6.1%. These
changes will be rolled into the quarterly
estimates when the Q3 national
accounts are released on Dec 2.
• Partial data suggests Q3 has seen a lift
in nominal spend although most of this

is a price effect. The ABS household
spending indicator, which covers around
two thirds of the total consumption
basket, showed a 2.1%qtr rise in nominal
terms in Q3 up from 1.3%qtr in Q2.
However, all of that was due to stronger
price growth with volumes posting a
0.7% rise in line with Q2 (see here).
• Our Westpac-DataX Card Tracker Index
foreshadowed the spending indicator
result and suggests quarterly growth
momentum is still tracking at around 2%

in mid-Oct in nominal terms. That said,
quarterly growth rate is likely slowing in
real terms given our estimates that Oct
saw a further step-up in price growth.
The latest monthly pace of gains also
looks to be about flat in nominal terms
(see Chart 8).
• Private sector business surveys point
to weak conditions for retail (consistent
with under-performing discretionary
goods segments) and stalling activity
for consumer services. Near term
expectations are at similar levels for

both but with year-weak expectations
flat rather than negative for retail.
• The ABS will publish full spending
estimates with the Q3 national accounts
on Dec 2. Based on partials we expect
these to show a solid +0.8%qtr gain,
lifting annual growth to 2.1%yr, an
upgrade on the +0.4%qtr, 1.5%yr
forecast in or last report. However, that’s
expected to be the high water mark for
growth with forecast to slow abruptly
into year end and early 2027, growth

tracking back to 1.4%yr by Q3 2027.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
9
Interest rates: hike fears realised
SENTIMENT INDICATORS
• Consumers have been on high alert for
interest rate rises for some time. Those
fears materialised in late Sep and again
in early Nov. The Oct survey was in the
field during the week of the Sep move.
Not surprisingly, the Westpac–Melbourne
Institute Mortgage Rate Expectations

Index, which tracks consumer interest
rate expectations, rose 5.5% to 179, just
shy of May’s cycle peak of 181.
• Amongst those surveyed after the RBA
decision, just over 80%, an overwhelming
majority, expect mortgage rates to
increase further over the next 12mths.
This compares to 64% in Sep. Across
the mortgage belt, the share is closer to
90%, with over 40% of this sub-group
expecting rates to rise by more than a
percentage point over the next twelve

months.
• While the Sep and Nov interest rate
increases were widely expected, the
scale of the rise over the last 12mths,
a full 1.25%, has come as a surprise.
This time last year, consumers were in
two minds, 39% of those with a view
expecting interest rates to rise and 37%
expecting them to fall. Only 20% of
consumers expected mortgage rates to
rise by over 1ppt.
• As at Oct, just over 40% of consumers
with a view expected mortgage interest
rates to rise by more than 1ppt. While

some hawkishness is clearly warranted,
this is extreme – it would take the
standard variable mortgage rate well
above 10%, to levels not seen since the
mid-1990s, thirty years ago.
• Overall, Oct responses suggest
consumers were broadly expecting
another three 25bp rate hikes from
the RBA. The Nov survey, currently in
the field, will capture how expectations
have shifted after the RBA’s more recent
move. While this may have amplified
rate rise fears, the split vote and some

indications from the Governor that there
may be a pause to assess things in Dec
could work the other way (see here for
more).
• The near term inflation outlook
clearly remains challenging. However,
Westpac expects the RBA to leave
rates on hold through the remainder
of 2026. And while there are risks
of a further tightening in early 2027
we think rates will more likely be
maintained at current levels with an
eventual gradual relaxation in policy
beginning in the second half of 2027.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
10
Inflation: still too hot but wages flat
SENTIMENT INDICATORS
•  Inflation has kicked up again over
the last 3mths, led by a resurgence
in global energy prices. Near term
consumer inflation expectations have
risen sharply again as well although
consumer wage expectations have not
moved, if anything moving marginally

lower in the latest month.
• The Sep CPI report showed headline up
1.3%qtr, the annual pace holding at the
Jun qtr pace of 3.9%yr. The key ‘trimmed
mean’ measure lifted 1.0%qtr, the
annual pace ticking up from 3.6%yr to
3.7%yr. The detail revealed underlying
inflationary pressures continuing even
while a moderation in housing inflation
(dwellings in particular) is unfolding
slightly faster than anticipated. Near-
term risks for inflation are to the upside

but more balanced further out (see here)
• Consumer inflation expectations
have moved higher again. After
peaking at 5.9% in Apr, the Melbourne
Institute’s ‘symmetric mean’ measure
of consumers’ year-ahead inflation
expectations eased back to 4.7% in Jul,
ticking up slightly to 4.9% in Aug and
Sep but jumped again back to 5.3%
in Jul. Expectations are likely being
skewed by the jump in fuel prices.
• Scaling according to historical inflation
outcomes suggests current expectations

are consistent with inflation of around
4%yr. It is difficult to look through the
effects of fuel price volatility, which
amplified expectations in Mar and Apr
but dampened them again when the
temporary halving in fuel excise tax
came into effect through Apr-May.
• From the RBA’s perspective, it will be
important for inflation expectations
to track back towards historical levels
in a timely manner as a sign that
expectations more generally remain

firmly anchored at low inflation rates.
• Certainly there are no signs that the
latest lift in inflation is becoming
embedded into expectations for wages.
Consumer assessments of actual wages
lifted a little when the new Award
decision came into effect but have
settled back to 1.4%, a touch above the
Jun qtr avg of 1.2% but below the long
run avg of 1.8%. Expectations have
tracked a slightly softer path, dipping
to 1.2% in Oct, down from a Jun qtr avg

of 1.3% and long run avg of 1.3%. At
face value, expectations are consistent
with a significant deterioration in real
wages.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
11
Durables: soft spend, dire sentiment
SENTIMENT INDICATORS
• Durables spend has been mixed 
over the first half of 2026, posting 
a  surprisingly solid gain in Q2 but 
with most of this centred on new 
vehicle spend where ‘extenuating 
circumstances’ were at play. Even 
excluding this, the flat underlying trend 
is not nearly as weak as the dire reads 
on the ‘time to buy a major item’ sub-
index would suggest. The last 3mths 
has seen the sub-index drop back 
again, a 4.3% decline taking it to 83, 
near the lows seen earlier in the year 
and a full 40pts below the long-run avg 
of 123. 
• As discussed regularly over the last four 
years, the extreme weakness appears 
to relate to the collapse in purchasing 
power since 2022. While prices have 
stabilised somewhat, their still high 
level is casting a dark shadow over 
buyer sentiment. 
• As Chart 18 shows, durable goods prices 
have been more stable since 2024 but 
look to have posted a bigger rise in the 
Sep quarter, our estimates based on 
the CPI detail pointing to annual price 
growth lifting back above 2.1%yr.

• There are a range factors at play here 
for prices. Construction costs have fed 
into the renovation segment of our 
measure. Some major durable items also 
have higher transport costs components. 
However, a notable medium term trend 
has been the absence of trend price 
declines for audio-visual and computing 
equipment, which make up a large part 
of the ‘small durables’ segment. 
• As chart 15 shows, growth in real 
per capita spend on the ‘major item’ 
segments centred on a 10%qtr jump in 
vehicles in Q2, led by strong take-up 
of EVs that was partly in response to 
the sharp move higher in fuel prices 
this year. Excluding vehicles, per capita 
spend was up just 0.3%qtr, 0.8%yr. 
• Partial data suggests Q3 is softer with 
the industry data pointing to a much 
slower 1%qtr gain for vehicle sales 
and the ABS Household Spending 
Indicator detail showing flat volumes for 
furnishings and household equipment. 
Our card data also suggests there 
has been a further shift to outright 
weakness in discretionary goods in the 
Oct month.

Dwellings: pessimistic and uncertain
SENTIMENT INDICATORS
•  Housing-related sentiment has been 
understandably unsettled in recent 
months, reflecting the heightened 
uncertainty around interest rate and 
tax policy changes amid evidence of a 
broadening downturn in established 
markets. 
• Assessments of ‘time to buy’ – 
generally seen as being from the 
perspective of owner occupiers rather 
than investors – have been choppy 
month to month but still in firmly 
pessimistic territory, albeit off the 
extreme lows seen in previous years. 
• The ‘time to buy a dwelling’ index 
actually lifted 3.6% over the 3mths to 
Oct sharply in May (–16%) but tracked 
a volatile monthly path, up 12% at one 
stage but unwinding most of that as 
inflation and rate rise fears returned. 
• At 88.4, the Oct index read is still far 
from positive, over 30pts below the 
long run avg of 119.

• The sub-group detail is also quite 
unsettled. The Oct month improvement 
was more pronounced for outright 
homeowners, retirees, 25–34-year-olds 
and those earning over $80k a year. 
But on a quarterly basis the biggest 
improvement has been amongst those 
aged 50+ and earning over $100k. 
• Interestingly, existing owners of 
residential property investments (25% 
of all respondents) continue to be 
outright positive on ‘time to buy’.
•  By state, buyer sentiment is still a little 
less downbeat in NSW and Vic (both 
state indexes at 93), where dwelling 
prices have seen bigger moves lower, 
but more pessimistic in Qld (85) and 
deeply pessimistic in WA (71) and SA 
(70). 
• The overarching question is whether 
improving affordability – reflecting a 
mix of lower prices and higher interest 
rates – will be sufficient to offset 
unease about the pace of price declines.

13
House prices: uncertain vs downbeat
SENTIMENT INDICATORS
• The broadening and deepening 
downturn in Australia’s housing 
markets is seeing an equally rapid shift 
in consumer house price expectations 
although these have not turned as 
negative as they did during the 2022-23 
correction. 
• The Westpac Melbourne Institute House 
Price Expectations Index declined a 
further 2.4% over the 3mths to Oct, 
following a steep 25% drop over the 
previous 3mths. At 115.6, the Oct index 
read is miles below the 15yr high of 
174 at the start of the year and below 
the long run avg of 130. 
• That said, the read is still above 100, 
meaning more consumers expect prices 
to rise than fall. This compares to the 
low of 91 during the 2022-23 correction 
when the consensus view was for 
continued declines. The Nov survey 
update will be an important gauge 
of whether a negative consensus has 
again emerged about the house price 
outlook. If it does, price momentum is 
likely to remain weak as buyers will be 
more inclined to hold off and sellers 
more willing to accept low prices now.

• The detailed responses in Oct were a 
picture of uncertainty, 46% of those 
surveyed expecting prices to rise, 18% 
expecting no change, 31% expecting 
prices to decline and 5% reporting 
“don’t know”. This was a similar mix to 
Jul.  
• That said, there is little doubt that 
the downturn is deepening. The 
Cotality home value index – covering 
all of Australia – fell 1.2% in Oct, 
annual growth dropping into negative 
(–2.8%yr) for the first time and prices 
down 6.7% from their peak in Mar (see 
here for a full run down). 
• Actual price growth has been broadly 
comparable to consumer house price 
expectations overall (see Chart 18), 
although the latest monthly result 
suggests the latter is set to move 
materially lower. By state, expectations 
have overshot outcomes in NSW and 
Vic, been very closely aligned in Qld but 
undershot to date in WA. 
• Notably, the sub-group detail shows 
price expectations have outright 
pessimistic (sub-100) for older age 
groups.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
14
Risk aversion: bracing
SENTIMENT INDICATORS
•  Consumers remain intensely risk 
averse. The Westpac Consumer Risk 
Aversion Index remained near historical 
extreme highs in Sep, dipping slightly 
from 60.1 to 57.5, but unchanged in 
seasonally adjusted terms (as shown 
on Chart 20). Readings are just a touch 
of the 52-year high of 60.8 recorded in 
June 2023.  
•  Recall that the index is based on 
responses to questions on the ‘wisest 
place for savings’. These ask consumers 
to nominate from a range of options, the 
risk index being the difference between 
the share nominating ‘safe’ options such 
as ‘deposits’ and ‘repay debt’ and the 
share nominating ‘risky’ options such as 
‘shares’ and ‘real estate’. 
• ‘Safe options’ remain very heavily 
favoured: 33% of consumers 
nominating ‘bank deposits’; 24% 
nominating ‘pay down debt’ and 7% 
nominating ‘superannuation’. The share 
nominating ‘real estate’ hold near 
historic lows at just 4.7%.

•  As noted previously, the mix of high 
inflation, interest rate rises, tax policy 
changes, a housing downturn, and 
numerous other factors – including 
conflict in the Middle East, tariffs, 
and a range of disruptions from AI 
– is creating an extremely uncertain 
environment for consumers when it 
comes to financial decisions. 
• This ‘safety first’ mode is typically 
associated with more elevated savings 
rates, increased voluntary debt 
repayments and an aversion to new 
borrowing that drives debt to income 
ratios lower over time. Notably, mix 
of responses on ‘repay debt’ suggests 
the focus is primarily on non mortgage 
rather than mortgage debt.
• The survey detail showed a fair bit 
of variation across sub-groups, with 
material declines in Vic and SA, 35-44 
year olds, retirees and males but rises 
in NSW and amongst 45-54 year olds. 
Note that all of these reads were taken 
from the Sep survey and so pre-date 
the RBA’s rate rise in late Sep.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
15
Job security: a wind shift?
SENTIMENT INDICATORS
• For Australian consumers, the labour 
market remains an island of relative 
calm amid the swirling mix of cost-of-
living stresses, rate rise and housing 
downturns. Job-loss fears have moved 
around, most recently rising again over 
the last three months, but the moves 
have been mild compared to wider 
sentiment swings and the overall level 
of concern still relatively mild.  
• The Westpac-Melbourne Institute 
Index of Unemployment Expectations 
rose 9.3% over the three months to 
Oct, reversing most of the 12.1% 
improvement over the previous three 
months (recall that higher reads mean 
more consumers expect unemployment 
to rise over the year ahead). At 142.2, 
the Oct index read is now decisively 
above the long run avg of 129 again but  
still well below the levels that would 
sound more worrying alarms.
• The latest ABS labour force figures 
show employment gains have been 
lumpy month to month but have 
averaged +34k/mth through May-Aug, a 
tick up from the +22k/mth average over 
the six months prior.

• Annual employment growth has 
been slower, holding within the 1.0-
1.5%yr range over the past year or 
so. With the working age population 
expanding 1.7%yr, the mix has seen 
the unemployment rate drift higher, 
reaching 4.7% in Aug vs 4.4% in 
May (see here for more). While there 
is evidence of a little more slack 
emerging in labour markets this 
appears to be mainly coming from a 
combination of increased supply and 
more moderate growth in hiring rather 
than retrenchments. Unfortunately 
changes to the ABS survey mean official 
estimates of both retrenchments and 
expected retrenchment (as shown in 
Chart 26) are unavailable.    
• Based on historical outcomes and 
adjusting for biases, the latest index 
read is roughly consistent with a 0.4-
0.6ppt rise in the unemployment rate, 
which would take it into the 5.2-5.3% 
range by this time next year.
• The sub-group detail shows real 
estate and construction workers led 
the deterioration in Oct, suggesting it 
is linked to interest rate and housing 
market developments.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
16
Queensland
•   The last time we profiled consumer
sentiment in Qld, a year ago, it was
seeing a marked divergence with
the rest of Aus with Qlders much
more pessimistic. Some of that
shift appeared to be linked to the
withdrawal of what were more material
cost-of-living supports provided by
the Qld state government, and an

associated sharper rise in headline
inflation. That in turn suggested
the negative wedge on sentiment
could close up a little. And for a time
this was the case: Qld more-or-less
re-converged sentiment-wise with
the rest of Aus around the start of
2026. However, since then, sentiment
amongst the state’s consumers has
deteriorated more sharply again. At just
76.3 in Oct, the ‘Sunshine state’ is the
gloomiest of the big 3 eastern states
and plumbing 2022-23 lows.

• Component-wise, the 6pt gap with the
rest of Aus in recent months has mainly
centred on differing assessments of the
economic outlook where there has been
a 7-8pt wedge. However, assessments
of finances deteriorated particularly
sharply in the Oct month suggesting the
housing downturn, which came on later
in Qld, may be a factor now as well.
• Qld’ers are also more pessimistic
on time to buy questions (especially
dwellings) but interestingly are less

troubled about unemployment.
• The latter may speak to a different
take on the wider economic and
inflation challenges: as a state, Qld
has tended to see stronger demand
growth (including an expected lift in
infrastructure and Olympics-related
build) that it putting more pressure
on capacity, particularly across its
labour market. Qld’s unemployment
rate is sitting at 4.5% a touch below
the national rate which is unusual
for a state that has tended to see

unemployment track slightly above the
national rate for most of its history (not
because of labour demand issues but
because the strength and composition
of its population growth has tended to
see more ample supply. This excessive
fear factor may be a view in Qld that
Australia’s capacity and inflation
problems may be more substantive and
enduring.
• Looking ahead, housing may be the
next area to weigh more heavily on
sentiment with prices in the state now

showing a very abrupt slowing (p13).

Document 458: United States _ A shock-resistant expansion (2026-09-24)

economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoPerspectives / 3rd Quarter 2026 / September 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
A SHOCK-RESISTANT EXPANSION
US growth remains resilient despite successive shocks. According to our forecasts, it is expected to reach 2.2% in 2026 and
2.3% in 2027. Its main driver is still investment in artificial intelligence (AI), which has remained entirely unaffected by the
energy crisis. The labour market has seen a marked improvement in job creation and a fall in the unemployment rate. Core

inflation remains stubbornly high and above the Federal Reserve (Fed)’s target. Against this backdrop, the Fed has begun a
hiking cycle in September, which, according to our scenario, would bring the Fed Funds target to 4.50% (upper limit) by Q1
2027. At the same time, long-term rates are under upward pressure.
1  This includes: energy and communications; data centres (facilities); IT equipment and peripherals; communication equipment (equipment); software; and research and develop-

ment (intellectual-property products).
THE ENERGY SHOCK IS NOT HOLDING BACK GROWTH
Driven by AI-related investment, US growth is holding up despite ri -
sing energy inflation. We forecast GDP growth at 2.2% in 2026 and
2.3% in 2027 on an annual average basis. The AI-related investment
cycle1 (+14.8% y/y in H1 2026) is expected to continue, accompanied by
potential productivity gains. However, other private-investment seg -
ments (including residential property) remain sluggish. In addition, the

rising cost of capital could accentuate its ‘K-shaped’ profile. Household
consumption is expected, on the whole, to benefit from the rebound in
job creation and to remain above 2% y/y through to the end of 2027.
In H1 2026, it held up against resurging inflation due to an average
year-on-year increase of 11.5% in tax refunds and a savings rate stan-
ding at its lowest level since 2022 (2.8% in Q2).
RECORD CAPITAL-RAISING DRIVEN BY AI
This year, AI-related investments are driving massive capital-raising

by companies through loans, bond issues or share placements. This
trend is set to continue given the seemingly colossal scale of the fun -
ding requirements, even as risk premia in the bond market begin to
widen. According to their statements, the five tech giants could invest
nearly USD 900 bn this year and over USD 1,000 bn next year—more
than double the figure for 2025. Spurred on by regulatory easing, the
major US banks have announced that they will mobilise significant re-

sources to support projects considered strategic for the United States
(AI, defence, energy and infrastructure). While funding for the AI boom
appears unaffected by rising long-term interest rates, these latter are
likely to continue to weigh on household demand for mortgages. Since
the start of the conflict in the Middle East, 30-year mortgage rates
have risen by 78 bp (to 6.76% at the start of September). Conversely,
consumer lending remained buoyant in H1 2026 and is supporting

household spending.
TWO-SPEED GROWTH IN INDUSTRY
Industrial production rebounded slightly in 2025 (+1.1%), driven by AI
and aerospace. We forecast an acceleration to +1.5% in 2026 and +2%
in 2027. Investment in AI is bolstering the electronics sector, while the
(civil and military) aerospace sector is rebounding as order backlogs
are cleared and defence requirements rise. Conversely, sectors ex -
posed to rising tariffs (in particular, the steel, aluminium and auto -

motive sectors) are still on the back burner, contrary to the objective
of these measures. This positive momentum in industrial production,
while concentrated, is expected to continue, bolstered by the recove -
ry in business sentiment. The ISM Manufacturing Index has been in
expansionary territory since early 2026, despite ongoing pressures on
prices and supply chains.

world
LABOUR MARKET: A LOW UNEMPLOYMENT RATE ON A DOWNWARD
TREND
Job creation (non-farm payrolls) has rebounded, averaging +80,000 per
month since the start of the year, compared with +10,000 in 2025. This
growth is no longer limited to the healthcare sector. Excluding health-
care, job creation is up by an average of +38,000 in 2026, compared

with -47,000 in 2025. Manufacturing and construction, two sectors
driven by AI, are contributing to this rebound. At the same time, the
labour force has not grown since January 2025 due to the decline in the
foreign-born workforce (down 4.2% cumulatively over the period). Ex -
cluding the impact of COVID-19, the labour force participation rate is at
its lowest level since the 1970s, standing at around 61.5%. The rebound
in labour demand and persistent pressure on the labour supply have

brought the unemployment rate down to 4.1% in August 2026 (a 0.3 pp
fall since the start of the year). The jobless rate is expected to fall to
3.9% at the end of 2027 (4.2% corresponds to full employment, accor -
ding to the CBO) thanks to a job creation rate of around 40,000–60,000
per month.
INFLATION: STRUCTURALLY ABOVE TARGET
The rise in oil prices has triggered a rebound in CPI inflation. The
contribution from the energy component (+1.2 pp) largely accounts for

the rise in inflation since February. A slowdown was seen (from +4.2%
to +3.4% y/y) between the May peak and August. This is expected to
continue due to base effects on energy prices. We anticipate the trough
to be +1.8% y/y in Q2 2027 before a stabilisation at around +2.3% y/y
later in the year.
Meanwhile, core inflation is expected to remain above the Fed’s 2%
target, and above +2.5% y/y in 2027. This highlights the structural
nature of inflation, beyond short-term fluctuations linked to energy.

Non-housing services are expected to show significant inertia, with in-
flation hovering at around +3% y/y by the end of 2027. The potential for
disinflation also appears limited for housing-related services, as the
rise in rents shows no signs of abating. Finally, inflation on non-energy
goods is slowing as the effects of tariffs fade. However, the AI cycle is
exerting upward pressure through the general rise in the cost of elec -
tronic goods.
FED: GREEN LIGHT FOR RATE HIKES

The FOMC has embarked on a cycle of rate increases. The Fed Funds
target was raised to 4.0% (+25 bp) at the September meeting, and we
anticipate two further rises that would bring the policy rate to 4.5% in
January 2027. These three rises (made possible by reassuring develop-
ments in employment and economic activity) are intended to reverse
the three cuts in 2025 adopted to counter the ‘downside risks’ to the
labour market at that time. As a result, the committee can recalibrate

its policy towards ‘price stability’ and, through its interest rate deci -
sions, reflect the priority given to this objective in its communications.
The risks point towards further monetary tightening: in our baseline
scenario, core inflation would remain sticky and the fall in unemploy -
ment would give the FOMC room for manoeuvre to raise rates further.
PUBLIC FINANCES: UNDERLYING DETERIORATION AND PRESSURE ON
LONG- TERM RATES
According to our estimates, the federal deficit is set to widen by 0.7 pp

to -6.6% of GDP in 2026 (by 0.4 pp to -3.1% for the primary deficit),
weighed down by falling tariff revenue (refunds of tariffs already col -
lected and lower-than-expected receipts). In 2027, the end of this one-
off effect is expected to lead to a marginal improvement to -6.3% (to
-2.8% for the primary deficit). The growing burden of interest payments
(3.5% of GDP in 2026 and 3.6% in 2027, following 3.2% in 2025) and
the conflict in Iran (affecting the military budget) are weighing on the

budget balance and federal-debt dynamics. Federal debt is still on an
upward trend, with no particular change in direction, exceeding 100% of
GDP (101.9%, up 3.3 pp, in 2026, and 103.5% in 2027). It is expected to
reach 110% by the end of the decade against a backdrop of budget de-
ficits of close to 6% of GDP , assuming fiscal policy remains unchanged.
Furthermore, the mid-term elections (which see the entire House of
Representatives and one-third of the Senate up for re-election) could

result in a divided Congress or in both chambers changing hands to
the Democratic Party. Should the former occur, this would increase the
risk of a legislative deadlock, while the presidential veto would limit
the Democrats’ ability to influence fiscal policy in the latter scenario.
Against this backdrop, pressure on the bond market is likely to continue
in the coming months. Treasury issuance is rising, fuelled by increa -
sing spending requirements (in areas such as AI, defence and supply

chains). At the same time, shifts in investor demographics are increa-
sing demand volatility, and issuances by companies in the artificial
intelligence sector are intensifying competition for capital. We antici -
pate a 10-year yield of 4.95% in Q1 2027, before a slight easing brought
about by Fed rate rises, which would lend credibility to the price-sta -
bility objective. However, according to our forecast, 10-year yield is
expected to remain close to a 20-year high, at 4.80% in Q4 2027.

THE US RELIES ON IMPORTS FOR ITS GROWTH
The US trade deficit has recently started to rise again. According to
our forecasts, it is expected to account for the same proportion of GDP
in 2026 as it did in 2024 (4.4%). This trend is driven by imports of
AI-related capital goods, which are set to rise from 1.2% to 2.1% of GDP
between 2022 and 2026. Investment in AI is, in fact, import-intensive
(Chart 2), which dampens its positive impact on growth and highlights

the US’s dependence on foreign supplies in a cycle that it is neverthe-
less spearheading.
The additional tariffs have failed to reverse the trend in the trade de -
ficit. Furthermore, the IEEPA tariffs struck down by the Supreme Court
were replaced, in July 2026, by ‘Section 301’ tariffs (forced labour) ran-
ging from 10% to 12.5%. These tariffs target 60 countries and have
been introduced on top of existing sector-specific tariffs (steel, alumi -

nium and automotive). Following these adjustments, the statutory rate
(measured against the 2024 import structure) remained slightly above
10%. The main risks relate to potential new sector-specific tariffs and
the renegotiation of the USMCA.
Article completed on 17 September 2026

Document 478: What’s the Renminbi Got to Do With it_ (2026-10-11)

Source PDF: all report pdf/What’s the Renminbi Got to Do With it_ (2026-10-11).pdf
Report source: CFR (Follow the Money)
Date: 2026-10-11
Pages: 17
Status: ok

### Page 1

1
EXPERT TAKE FOLLOW THE MONEY
What’s the Renminbi Got to Do With it?
A closer look at the impact of China’s currency on China’s export growth.
Sean Yong/Reuters
UPDATED
October 11, 2026 12:00 a.m.
Articles Follow the Money Share
Log In
Regions Topics Research Experts Events The Future of American Strategy

### Page 2

2
Brad W. Setser CFR EXPERT
Whitney Shepardson Senior Fellow
There is an argument—made in the recent Geneva
Report and by various former IMF officials—that
China’s current export surge is the simple
consequence of the housing downturn and the
resulting weakness in Chinese internal demand.*
The weakness in the RMB in this story is entirely
incidental to the increase in exports, which is, more or
less, a mechanical response to weak internal demand.

Cannot sell excavators at home? Export them.
Built more modern car capacity than your internal
market supports? Export that too…
The fall in the nominal RMB over the course of 2022
and the first half of 2023 in nominal and read terms
was simply a function of domestic macroeconomic
weakness, and a growing rate differential. It wasn’t an
active policy choice by China’s central bank.

### Page 3

3
Now, some might note that China’s currency doesn’t
always move with rate differentials. The RMB didn’t
consistently get pulled up when Chinese rates were
higher than U.S. rates (2002-2020 generally
speaking), and while it did fall when rate differentials
moved against the RMB in 2022 and 2023, it then
stabilized and has even appreciated modestly since
the middle of last year. That appreciation hasn’t been
big enough to correct the deep undervaluation of the

RMB, but it came at a time when the PBOC policy
rate (the reverse repo rate) was well below the U.S.
policy rate.
China doesn’t have an open financial account. As a
result, equilibrium in the foreign exchange market is
thus driven far more by exporter conversion and
current account flows than by rate differentials and
financial flows.** That, together with China’s ongoing
management of the exchange rate and a trade surplus
that can generate significant inflows, means that the

### Page 4

4
value of the RMB is much more of a policy variable
under the independent control of the Chinese
authorities than a simple byproduct of monetary and
fiscal policies.
The modern IMF is reluctant to accept this
conclusion. Its surveillance has generally ignored
China’s currency management (even if that
management is relatively obvious; see the data on FX
settlement) and its recent comprehensive assessment
of China’s economy completely ignored the balance of

payments and exchange rate management.
But it is pretty obvious to the market, even if the
PBOC, rather absurdly, insists that “market forces
play a decisive role in determining the RMB
exchange rate.”
I doubt the PBOC can win this argument. I agree
with Mark Sobel:
“The renminbi is still heavily managed and the
authorities have acted to slow appreciation. The paper
states that the PBoC phased out ‘regular’ foreign
exchange intervention in 2017. But it has many

means at its disposal to achieve the same effect
through extensive capital controls, administrative
guidance, signalling at the fix and deploying state-
owned commercial banks. These means are heavily
used. Thus, the word ‘regular’ must be taken with a
pinch of salt. The comparison to Japan, which

### Page 5

5
intervenes occasionally, is spurious – the yen largely
floats freely in integrated global markets. The same
cannot be said for the renminbi.”
In reality, the PBOC’s daily fix now plays the decisive
role in the currency’s path, and the market is often a
bet on how the PBOC’s management of the yuan will
evolve.
That is clear from the lack of volatility in the yuan’s
rate against the dollar. The yuan’s move down against
the dollar in 2022 was a controlled 10 percent move,

and the appreciation over the last 16 months has been
even more controlled crawl.
And the market is clearly massaged with substantial
help from the state banks. In fact that the pattern of
changes in the monthly FX settlement data looks
exactly like the pattern of intervention of a central
bank managing its currency around a tight band (the

### Page 6

6
state banks in practice buy FX to keep the CNY from
testing the strong side of the band, so there is an
asymmetry in how the CNY is managed)
None of this would matter much if China’s economy
was small. But that obviously isn’t the case. Nor would
this matter much if China’s exports didn’t respond to
changes in the RMB’s real (and nominal) value. But
that is also not the case.
There is abundant evidence that China’s exports do
respond to moves in the currency. Periods of RMB

weakness have consistently been associated with
periods of especially rapid Chinese export growth.

### Page 7

7
Chancellor Merz and President Macron got this
right, even if macroeconomic issues haven’t yet
figured prominently in the EU’s dialogue with
China.*** As they note in their open letter to the
European Commission:
“An appreciation of undervalued currencies towards a value
more in line with fundamentals would help support the
rebalancing of trade accounts with the EU in the short and
medium term … European policy discussions focused on
forging a common understanding of these exchange rate issues

and their interaction with global imbalances would be useful.”
The best evidence that exports responded to changes
in the currency, and not simply to changes in the path
of China’s internal demand, is the acceleration in
China’s export growth after the RMB’s depreciation
over the course of 2022.
This is the killer chart.

### Page 8

8
Now, you might ask: what of the acceleration in
export growth in 2020 and 2021? That was pretty
clearly tied to the global increase in goods demand
that came with the pandemic and work from home
requirements. It was expected to fade when the world
economy normalized. But it didn’t. By 2023 Chinese
export growth again topped global trade growth.
The general expectation that the rise in China’s goods
surplus during Covid would prove to be “transitory”

### Page 9

9
heavily influenced the IMF’s work at the time: see the
IMF’s external sector reports of 2023 and 2024.
I would argue that neglecting the exchange rate was
the key reason the IMF’s kept forecasting, incorrectly,
that China’s current account surplus would fall (See
this blog with Shahin Vallee). The IMF certainly
didn’t warn that China’s property downturn and the
RMB’s real depreciation risked a second China shock
in its 2022, 2023, or 2024 surveillance reports.

Concretely, the weaker RMB appears to have
contributed to a very large outperformance of China’s
exports relative to global trade. See the following
chart
That incidentally also happened after the RMB
followed the dollar down in 2003.
In both periods, there were significant structural
shifts. The WTO accession in 2002, and the success

### Page 10

10
of Chinese industrial policies in the “green” industrial
sectors and autos in 2022. In both cses exports were
turbocharged when structural shifts that should have
resulted in a stronger currency were instead amplified
by currency weakness.
No matter. Paul Krugman has noted that a country
that is converging technologically with its trade
partners should see a trend appreciation in its
currency. China got the technological convergence,
but not the real appreciation—at least not until this

year. And the move since the middle of 2025 has
corrected the depreciation of the RMB that occurred
when the RMB followed the dollar down on
liberation day, but not the structural overvaluation.
Now, there is an argument that exchange rate
adjustment is too hot an issue to make it a priority.
The PBOC isn’t interested it seems.
And some believe that in the interest of comity and
good relations with China, the IMF should mirror
the PBOC’s views. After all a stronger RMB would

slow China’s exports and thus slow China’s economy
at a time when China needs export growth to escape
deflation and generate employment. Or so the
argument goes. Concerns about “external balance“
and the negative global spillovers from China’s rising
goods surplus should be ignored and the Fund’s sole
focus should be on policies that lead to internal

### Page 11

11
balance. That provides one rationale for the Fund’s
ongoing decision to essentially ignore the contentious
issue of the PBOC’s ongoing exchange rate
management.
That argument, however, assumes that China’s
economy is small and thus China’s external surplus
can safely be ignored because it isn’t a drag on the
growth of the IMF’s other members.
That clearly isn’t true.
And it isn’t clear that China is more interested in
having a negotiation over its level of basic pension

benefits or the size of its fiscal deficit (where the
IMF continues to make timid recommendations that
would not move the needle—and certainly wouldn’t
meaningfully reduce China’s external surplus) than a
discussion over its currency practices.****
Back in the world of fixed but adjustable exchange
rates, there was an expectation (not always met to be
sure) that surplus countries would allow their
currencies to strengthen to bring their economies

back into external balance, and that other policy tools
would be used to ensure internal balance.
I would argue that China’s exchange rate (against
both the dollar and the basket) has many similarities
to the old world of fixed but adjustable currencies.
True, it moves on a controlled path when it moves

### Page 12

12
between different levels rather than jumping up and
down. But the PBOC does frequently target a level,
and that targeted level does change over time.
I would further argue that steps like the recent tariff
rate quota that will limit China’s exports of hybrid
cars to Europe to around 350,000 units next year (up
from 2025, but well below the 800,000 units of
2026) aren’t going to address the underlying
imbalance on their own. They can provide a bit of a

buffer for specific sectors.
But that buffer, while necessary, won’t address the
broad-based erosion of manufacturing capacity
outside China. This erosion will persist as long as an
undervalued RMB sends the entire world a signal to
use China as its production base.
The new system of managed trade that is emerging in
autos would need to be progressively expanded to an
ever-increasing set of sectors if protection is used as a
substitute for both domestic reform in China and

exchange rate adjustment.
That is now the direction of travel, but it doesn’t
address the underlying systemic issues.
Incidentally, those who believe the path of internal
demand is critical to rebalancing and that exchange
rate moves are secondary need to explain how an
acceleration in domestic demand would, on its own,

### Page 13

13
lead China’s external accounts to adjust. One
important, stylized fact about China is that domestic
demand growth is no longer feeding over to import
growth (ex-gold, that is).
Bottom line: China, which needs a 1.5 percentage
point contribution from net exports every year, is on a
collision course with its trading partners without a
currency correction that provides the basis for more
balanced trade even in the absence of trade
restrictions.
And there isn’t a realistic path towards balance that

doesn’t involve a stronger RMB; there aren’t, as Paul
Krugman once put it, immaculate transfers.
* That downturn shows up in domestic deflationary
pressure, and weak job growth/high rates of (at least
youth) unemployment. China, in other words, isn’t in
“internal balance.” I really liked the report from

### Page 14

14
Kamakshya Trivedi and Hui Shan of Goldman Sachs
arguing China needs two tools to hit two targets
(internal and external balance).
** To be sure, rate differentials matter for exporter
conversion—but so does the expected movement of
the RMB. The market understands this better than
the Fund. The Chen and Suwanapruti (Goldman
Sachs) note uses a framework similar to mine to
describe the CNY’s moves in Q3:
“In our view, sizeable FX inflows have continued to support

RMB appreciation despite unfavorable interest rate
differentials, as strong export receipts and increased exporter
FX conversions have outweighed portfolio outflows. Our
estimated FX conversion ratio rose to 63% in August,
compared with around 45% in normal years, likely reflecting a
shift in market expectations from RMB depreciation to
appreciation.”
*** Š ef č ovi č  has jurisdiction over trade policy
(narrowly defined), but not broader macroeconomic

policies. Europe’s institutional architecture for
exchange rate coordination is not well-developed.
**** The current account surplus is no longer masked
to the same extent as in 2023 and 2024, as SAFE has
fiddled with its BoP methodology once again (per the
Federal Reserve) and the BoP goods surplus is no
longer significantly below the customs goods surplus.
Of course, there are still problems in the data—the
deficit in the investment income account most

聯準會10月FOMC會議維持目標利率在3.75~4%區
間，但隨著通膨仍在升溫 ，目前市場普遍預期聯準
會在今年12月及2027年上半年將再各升息一碼。公
用事業屬於資本密集型產業 ，極度依賴債務融資來
支持基礎設施建設與更新 ，升息提高了企業利息支
出、直接壓縮利潤空間 ，對公用事業獲利與產業評
價相對不利。
近期隨著部分 AI與經濟敏感型股票面臨估值修正與
財報疑慮，市場資金開始撤出高基期板塊 ，轉向醫
療保健與公用事業等相對保守 、現金流穩健的防禦
型領域，但在目前利率仍高的情況下 ，預估短期內

公用事業走勢將呈區間內來回震盪。

零售客流量表
重點摘要
• 美國多州出現暫停資料中心建
設的政策訴求 ，資金可能加速
流向規則較為明確的州。
• 電力股的投資邏輯正在轉向 ，
未來將更看重獲利品質與監管
可預測性。
• AI資料中心電力需求仍是公用
事業3Q26財報重點 ，但焦點轉
向如何平衡供應瓶頸與監管成
本。
《部分電力股評價將更看重監管可預測性》
在2026年美國期中選舉涉及的36場州長選戰中，已
有20州出現暫停資料中心建設或許可的政策訴求 。
電價、用水與基礎設施成本的分攤爭議 ，正改寫各
州爭取AI投資的方向 ，資金可能從監管混沌的地區 ，
加速流向規則較為明確的州 。德州為這輪監管分化
中最值得關注 、也最棘手的地區 。德州電網接入申
請佇列已成長到約 474吉瓦，其中約九成來自資料

中心，而德州州長已宣布暫停核准新的資料中心接
入州內電網，9月底環境委員會的暫停令進一步擴及
自備電源的離網專案 ，意味企業即使自己發電 ，也
未必能繞過許可關卡。
相較之下，在大規模用電需求湧入前 ，先把監管制
度架好的州或許更具產業前景 。懷俄明、明尼蘇達、
愛荷華、威斯康辛 、內華達、南卡羅來納與田納西
州為潛在的資金承接者 。明尼蘇達已立法防止大客
戶把成本轉嫁給一般用戶 ，科羅拉多的大負荷費率
方案則要求至少15年合約、80%最低計費需求與提
前退出費 ，把部分退出風險交還給客戶 。電力股的
投資邏輯正在轉向 ，未來部分公用事業股的評價分
化，將從過去單純看需求規模 ，轉向更看重獲利品
質與監管可預測性。
《AI資料中心電力需求仍為近期財報重點 ，但焦點
轉向如何平衡供應瓶頸與監管成本》
隨著3Q26財報陸續披露，美國公用事業公司對AI資
料中心用電需求普遍釋出極為樂觀且迫切的長線成
長展望。以FirstEnergy為例，受惠於毗鄰北弗吉尼
亞數據中心走廊的優勢 ，西弗吉尼亞州等地的資料

中 心 相 關 負 載 需 求 激 增 137% 達到 4.3 吉瓦 。
American Electric Power其累積的增量負載承諾已
飆升至56吉瓦，半年內暴增一倍 。但公用事業高層
的態度在積極擴產中也帶有審慎風險控管 ，為了避
免新建發電廠及電網升級的費用侵蝕一般用戶權益 ，
業者正重新設計合約 ，要求科技公司簽署最低電費
與信用擔保條款 。整體而言 ，公用事業財報反映出
資料中心用電需求並未降溫 ，但產業焦點已從 「如
何接到訂單 」轉向「如何平衡供應瓶頸與監管成本 」
的階段。

7.5%，預估SS NOI淨有效基礎上調0.25%至5.5%-
6%。策略資本收入略降至 6.5-6.6億美元 ，資本部
署中上調開發案穩定 、新開工開發案 、收購，但利
率走升將壓抑物業交易活動及估值擴張 ，將放緩資
產處分以應對 ，但維持開發利潤金額 。產業新增供
給量放緩，預估今年FFO年增率約7-8%。

新約或續約租金變動率
市場空置率仍吃緊 ，EQIX加速推進資料中心建設項
目，預計今年總資本支出將達到 50-60億美元 ，現
年度總預訂額為 5.33億美元創新高 ，可在90天內轉

化為收入 ，新交易價格 YOY+12%，續約價也穩定
上漲，預計4Q26每月經常性收入成長10~11%，營

49-50%，預估4Q26調整後FFO約YOY+9%，雖看
好，但易受市場對 AI融資及變現疑慮壓抑估值擴張 。

《AI發債引發債務可持續性擔憂》
近期公債殖利率攀高引發市場對 AI融資需求以及
企業償債能力的擔憂 ，AI產業鏈信用利差與 CDS
擴大，不過多數大型科技企業多為投資級 ，美國
投資級AI產業鏈信用利差水準仍未突破今年高點；
此外統計今年以來大量發債的美國投資級債發行
人(淨發行量100億美元以上)共有17家，其中17%
的發行人信評獲調升 ，33%被下調 ，50%不變，
反映債務增加尚未造成普遍的債信壓力。

的風險溢酬 ，並非潛在的信用風險事件或整體信用
基本面惡化 ，不過仍須留意目前總體經濟環境對於
信用評級較低的發行人而言更容易受到長期高利率
和成本壓力的影響 ，高低評等發行人的市場表現分
歧或將加大。

不多，2026年迄今的評級上調 /下調比率為2.3倍；

投資級債整體信用環境維持良好。

惡化
《歐洲信用債市受法國財政疑慮波及》
歐洲信用市場雖受 AI相關債券波動影響較小 ，但因
能源價格高企 ，加上近期法國主權和財政風險加劇 ，
歐洲信用債市 十月份表現也有不小波動 ，尤其是非
投資級債信用利差一度擴大逾 60個基點。歐洲銀行
債是各產業信用債主權風險曝險較高的類別 ，因歐
洲銀行多數不及美國銀行營運遍及全球且業務多元 ，
此外也持有不少主權債資產 ，十月份市場表現也反
映此一狀況 ，主權債的波動更加重了歐洲銀行債的
跌幅，法國銀行債利差擴大幅度最為明顯 ，所幸法
國主要銀行包括法國巴黎銀行以及興業銀行3Q26獲

利狀況良好 ，即使經營環境惡化 ，從不良貸款率與
信用風險成本等資產品質指標來看法國巴黎銀行持
平前季、法國興業銀行僅微幅上升。
目前市場關注的焦點仍是法國財政整頓的路徑 ，法
國目前的債務負擔約佔 GDP的118%至119%，高盛
預計，如果沒有更有效的財政調整 ，到2030年初負
債比率將進一步上升至接近 125%，如要穩定債務
佔GDP的比重，法國需要實現約佔 GDP 1%的財政
盈餘，但目前的財政赤字約佔 2.5% GDP。法國政
府當局可能在年底前通過一項撙節的預算案 ，若沒
通過法國主權債與信用債可能進一步惡化 ，但即使
通過法國主權債利率要大幅回落的機會仍不大 ，可
能要到2027年法國大選後主權債才有機會趨於穩定 ，
屆時法國銀行債或有布局機會。
歐洲可供吸收損失銀行債信用利差(基點)
資料來源：高盛，2026/10，中國信託商業銀行整理，2026/10

重點摘要
• 高利率持續下信用基本面都
已 出 現分 化，如CCC等級的
償債能力指標顯著疲弱
• 衡量整體債信風險仍是歐洲
較美國為高
《非投資級債美國仍優於歐洲》
儘管目前不論是美國還是歐洲的非投資級債在高利
率持續下信用基本面都已出現分化 ，如CCC等級的
償債能力 指標顯著疲弱 ，但衡量整體債信風險仍是
歐洲較美國為高；原因包括歐洲的債務到期牆較美
國明顯，且美國非投資級債2028年前到期的債券分
佈在相對廣泛的行業 ，而當前的賤售債券主要集中
在媒體、原物料與電信 、非必需消費等特定領域 ，
上述較少重疊降低落入賤售債券利差區間的發行人
同時面臨債務到期後償債或再融資令違約風險增加
的可能。另一方面 ，歐洲非投資級債的到期牆更為

陡峭，且市場利率與2028年前到期債券的票面利率
差距大於美非投資級債 ，反映再融資壓力更鉅 ，尤
其是歐洲CCC等級。
分析歐洲非投資級債各產業債務到期狀況可發現
2028年前到期債務產業相對集中 ，且易受景氣影響
的非必需消費在到期債務與賤售債券都佔有一定比
例，另外槓桿率本就較高的電信業佔 2028年前到期
債務9%，融資成本大幅上漲恐令這些發行人債信體
質進一步惡化。
資料來源：高盛，2026/10，中國信託商業銀行整理，2026/10

分析師／林亞瑱
研究範疇／歐洲總經政策與市場分析
《財政與政治不確定性壓抑估值，歐股延續震盪》
市場對法國財政可持續性以及其可能產生的外溢擔
憂升溫，牽動十月以來歐股走勢震盪，銀行股跌勢
較重，所幸食品飲料與健護等防禦型產業 、能源類
股上揚支撐歐股收紅。評估目前情勢屬法國財政信
心危機，而非歐洲主權風險全面升溫；融資成本上
升對歐洲企業帶來壓力，然整體槓桿可控，財務體
質脆弱的企業面臨較大風險。隨著預算協商進行、
財政擔憂短線難消除，歐央12月利率會議可能再升
息一碼，公債殖利率預估將維持高位，加上中東地
緣風險未除，將對市場情緒與歐股評價帶來壓力 ，
預估歐股短線將延續震盪走勢，維持中立評等。

《法國財政疑慮、政治僵局與社會動盪導致法德利
差擴大，明年大選前政治風險溢酬預估維持高檔》
十月份法國公債殖利率大幅上升 ，反映法國政治
局勢分裂 、財政整頓前景不明 ，加上明年總統大
選恐由極右派勝出 ，市場對法國財政可持續性的
疑慮升高 ，法國十年期公債殖利率 10月下旬一度
突破5%，創2002年7月以來新高。

近期法國各地爆發大規模抗議活動 ，亦對市場帶
來衝擊 。法國政治與財政風險升溫 ，反而吸引資
金轉向德國公債避險 ，加上近期金融條件趨緊 、
市場對歐央升息的預期相較九月底降溫 ，推動德
國十年期公債殖利率自高檔回落 ，法德利差大幅
擴大 、10/22一度突破 170個基點 ，創2011年11
月歐債危機以來新高(統計至10/26)。
針對2027年預算 ，法國政府提出 430億歐元財政
整頓方案，目標將財政赤字占 GDP比率自2026年
的5.4%降至2027年的5%，措施包括凍結公部門
薪資、限制每月超過1,260歐元的退休金通膨調整
幅度、延長針對大型企業的特別稅但縮小適用範
圍等。反對黨已對多項措施表達反對 ，若不採取
任何樽節措施 ，2027年法國財政赤字占 GDP比率
預估將達6.5%。由於大選在即 ，從近期極右派國
民聯盟釋出的意向來看 ，雖不支持預算案 ，卻也
不願在大選前引發政治危機 ，預估現任總理勒克
努仍有機會透過援引 《憲法》第49.3條於年底前
通過一份財政整頓力度較有限的預算案 。然而，

即使預算案過關，也難扭轉法國債務攀升趨勢。

重點摘要
3
近來融資成本 上升進一步加重法國的債務負擔 。
信評機構 惠譽(2026/10/12)上調利息支出預測 ，
預估2026年至2028年間，利息支出將累積佔GDP
的0.8%。利息支出占GDP比率預估將由2026年約
2.5%升至 20 28年約3.3%，2030年進一步升至
3.8%，反映高融資成本對財政的壓力將逐步顯現 。
利息支出增加將壓縮其他公共支出及財政空間 。
若赤字改善幅度不如預期 ，債務比率恐持續上升 。
2027年總統大選亦將牽動法國財政政策與債市走
向。法國總統大選預計於 2027年4至5月舉行，第
一輪投票訂於 4/18，若無候選人取得過半選票將
於5/2進行第二輪投票。最新民調顯示，極右派國
民聯盟候選人勒龐在首輪支持度領先 ，超過三成；

極左派法國不屈黨候選人梅朗雄近期支持度上升 ，
引發市場擔憂將發生沒有中間派候選人進入第二
輪決選的不利情境。
梅朗雄主張擴張性財政政策 ，對市場最為不利 ，
勝選機率較高的勒龐 10/6提出財政整頓方案並且
主張建立一個可信的債務穩定框架 ，試圖安撫市
場對法國財政前景的疑慮 。在面對市場壓力下 ，
勒龐表達更具財政紀律的立場有助稍稍緩和財政
擴張的擔憂 ，但其財政整頓方案的政策假設與具
體實施存在不確定性 ，加上大選後很可能緊接著
進行國會改選 ，政治版圖維持分裂狀態不利財政
改革。
資料來源：Fitch，2026/10/12，中國信託商業銀行彙整，2026/10。

重點摘要
4
整體而言 ，財政和政治不確定性預估將令法德利
差維持在高位 ，下檔情境為若 2027年預算案無法
於年底前通過 ，恐將面臨更大的財政惡化與市場
壓力，十年期法德利差恐有進一步擴大的風險。
《目前情勢屬法國財政信心危機，而非歐洲主權風
險全面升溫》
儘管投資人對法國債券要求更高的風險補償 ，但
近期法國公債標售仍有足夠的認購需求 。除此之
外，目前法德利差擴大對整體歐洲市場的外溢影
響尚屬可控 ，多數歐洲國家相對德國利差雖受牽
動近期亦出現較明顯的擴大 ，但利差水準仍可控 。
以政府債務占 GDP比重較高的義大利來看 ，十年
期義德利差 10/22一度升至 124個基點 ，回升至
2025年4月水準，但仍遠低於 2011年歐債危機時

期。因此，目前情勢仍偏向法國自身的財政信心
危機，而非歐洲主權信用風險全面升溫。
資料來源：Bloomberg，2024年來至2026/10/21，中國信託商業銀行彙整，2026/10。

• 10月歐元區 PMI維持擴張但

《歐元區PMI維持擴張但成長放緩，通膨壓力面臨
上行風險》
歐元區10月綜合PMI初值由9月的53.1降至51.9，
雖連續四個月高於 50、顯示經濟持續擴張 ，但成
長動能有所放緩 。法國受到大規模罷工與抗議活
動影響 ，商業活動下滑 ，成為主要拖累因素之一 ，
法國10月綜合PMI初值降至48.4，明顯落後其他
歐元區國家 。另一方面 ，能源及投入成本 上升迫
使企業提高售價，通膨壓力未明顯緩解。
中東緊張局勢延宕，布蘭特原油期貨價格升至116

美元/桶，TTF天然氣期貨價格升至96歐元/兆瓦時，
天然氣價格持續突破今年高點(統計至10/26)。
目前能源價格高於歐洲央行九月的基準與不利情
境預測 ，歐元區通膨面臨上行風險 ，不過未出現
明顯的二輪通膨效應 ，經濟成長則面臨下行壓力 ，
預期歐洲央行連續升息機率低 。此外，近期公債
殖利率上升亦發揮了緊縮效果 ，預估歐洲央行將
暫時按兵不動 。待12月份利率會議 ，屆時歐洲央
行將取得更新的經濟預測 ，可據此重新評估通膨
與成長前景 ，若能源價格及通膨壓力持續偏高 ，
不排除12月將再升息一碼 ，將存款機制利率調升
至2.75%。
針對近期主權債券殖利率大幅走升 ，預估歐洲央
行將密切關注其走勢 ，但長債利率的上升尚未達
到表明主權債券市場面臨顯著壓力的水平 ，應不
足以觸發政策調整。
資料來源：摩根士丹利，2026/10/22，中國信託商業銀行彙整，2026/10。

重點摘要
7
《融資成本上升對歐洲企業帶來壓力，然整體槓桿
率尚可控，財務體質脆弱的企業面臨較大風險》
主權債券市場的壓力令歐洲企業信用利差同步擴大，
自9月以來，歐洲投資等級債信用利差擴大31個基
點至108個基點，非投資等級債信用利差擴大幅度
較為顯著，擴大105個基點至363個基點，兩者均
回升至2025年5月以來高點(統計至10/26止)。所幸，
歐洲企業資產負債表整體仍維持穩健。儘管淨債務
/EBITDA比率自低檔回升，但整體槓桿水準與歷史
水準相比並不高，仍處健康水準。因此，債務成本
上升雖增加企業的財務負擔，尤其是財務體質較脆
弱的企業面臨較高的風險，但目前尚不至於構成系
統性威脅。另一方面，融資成本上升，企業獲利可

能面臨壓力 ，根據高盛證券 (10/23)預估，在其他
條件不變下，債務成本每上升100個基點，道瓊歐
洲600企業每股盈餘 (EPS)將減少約3%，其中財務
體質較脆弱的企業所受衝擊最大，具備強健資產負
債表與穩定利潤率的企業較能抵禦高利率影響。
《利率維持高位壓抑估值，歐股短線將延續震盪》
隨著預算協商進行、財政擔憂短線難消除，歐央12
月利率會議可能再升息一碼，公債殖利率預估將維
持高位，加上中東地緣風險未除，近期市場對美國
期中選舉後，美伊敵對行動可能重燃的預期升溫，
均將對市場情緒與歐股評價帶來壓力，預估歐股短
線將延續震盪走勢，所幸，LSEG I/B/E/S(10/22)統
計，道瓊歐洲600企業3Q26財報公布雖仍在初期階
段，但目前整體獲利優於財報季前預測，2026年企
業獲利預估相較10月初呈現上修趨勢，歐洲企業獲
利成長應有助提供下檔支撐，歐股維持中立評等。
資料來源：高盛證券，2026/10/23，中國信託商業銀行整理，2026/10。

《土耳其基金清算風波 、區域雜音增多 ，新歐展

未能滿足投資人贖回請求 ，土耳其資本市場委員
會下令清算七家資產管理公司管理的 131檔基金 ，
合計資產管理規模約 1兆里拉，MSCI 10/15宣布

凍結土耳其股票的部分指數調整措施 ，並警告若
土耳其未能改善市場誠信與透明度問題 ，最快將
於2027年6月考慮將其從新興市場降級 。考量地
緣風險未除 、土耳其基金清算問題造成的市場動
盪，維持新歐股市中立看法。
貨幣政策方面，9月波蘭CPI年增率升至4.1%，較
8月的3.4%上升且超過央行容忍區間上限 ，波蘭
央行10月利率會議維持政策利率於 3.75%不變。
中東地緣僵局持續 、能源價格維持高檔更久令通
膨前景存在上行風險 ，預估波蘭央行將更長時間
維持利率不變 。土耳其方面 ，10/22土耳其央行
宣布將一週附買回利率調降 100個基點 ，從37%
下調至36%，為時隔9個月再次降息，符合市場預
期，央行會後聲明顯示 ，儘管地緣政治局勢發展
令能源價格維持高檔可能對通膨前景帶來上行風
險，然而，土耳其國內需求疲軟 ，供給面衝擊對
國內價格的傳導效應有限 ，暗示有持續放鬆貨幣
政策限制性的空間。
土耳其基金九月份資產縮水390億美元
資料來源：摩根士丹利，2026/10/26，中國信託商業銀行整理，2026/10。

《高利高通膨壓抑，留意日股短線震盪加大》
10月日本股市大幅震盪修正 ，符合本行下調評等之
預期。其中，日經225指數在10月初一度上破7萬點
大館，此後因AI相關估值疑慮、科技股修正與長端利
率上行，全球科技股估值大幅修正，日經225指數在
10月23日盤中一度下探61,462點。
日本兩大指數在本月表現也呈現相對分化。日經225
權重權重集中於東京威力科創、愛德萬測試、軟銀集
團等半導體與 AI標的，在本波修正幅度較大；相反
的，TOPIX指數因金融、內需與防禦類股權重較高，
加上利率下行有利銀行以外的內需類股，回檔相對溫
和。也間接反映了10月的修正偏向是 「AI交易擁擠
的釋放」，而非全面性的景氣衰退定價。
債市方面，10年期日本公債殖利率在10月初一度升

至3.11%、創1999年以來新高 ，反映日本央行升
息、以及高市內閣財政擴張的雙重疑慮。
此後殖利率一路回落至 10月26日的2.82%、下行約
23個基點。這一下行幅度反映全球 AI相關信用風險
引發的避險需求。
重點摘要
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026/9，中國信託商業銀行整理

AI相關估值疑慮、科技股修正

但修正主要集中於半導體與AI
權值股。
• TOPIX相對抗跌，顯示市場仍
未全面反映景氣衰退，而是資
金自擁擠交易部位撤出。

《日本出口維持韌性，內需動能出現疑慮》
經濟基本面部分，日本9月出口在半導體與AI相關需
求帶動下，連續13個月維持正成長 ，惟增速放緩至
16.7%、略低於預期；但進口年增卻升至18.9%，主
要因為進口能源價格大漲所致，導致貿易逆差擴大，
外需雖強卻未能轉化為淨出口貢獻。
內需與生產端則收高利高通膨壓力同步降溫。工業生
產增速自年中高點回落，製造業PMI降至七個月來最
低，整體擴張速度已低於上半年水準，細項中新訂單
與出口訂單成長同步放緩，能源與勞動成本則大幅走
升，對企業營運形成壓力。內需改善亦相當有限，零
售銷售下滑、失業率微升、求人求職比回落，反映勞
動市場略有降溫；所幸，實質薪資已連續八個月正成
長、消費者信心小幅回升。

整體而言，日本經濟仍處於「名目成長、實質停滯」
的格局，擴張主要仰賴出口，內需復甦力道偏弱，而
高油價帶來的逆差壓力與生產動能轉弱，將是後續成
長的主要考驗 。基於地緣與油價的不確定性 ，10月
日本央行暫停升息，維持利率不變。
重點摘要
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026/10，中國信託商業銀行整理

口成本與貿易逆差。工業生產、
PMI及勞動市場同步降溫，經
濟仍維持「出口強、內需弱」
格局，成長動能面臨考驗。

《日銀暫停升息，12月升息預期支撐利率高檔》
日本央行已於 9月會期將政策利率上調 25個基點至
1.25%，為1995年以來最高 ，也是三個月內第二
次、1990年以來最快的升息節奏。
隨著近期經濟數據 ，10月份日央如預期暫停升息一
碼，會議同步公布《經濟與物價形勢展望報告》，根
據央行經濟預測顯示潛在通膨「已大致達到2%」。
配合植田和男在10月16日記者會曾表示，若基礎通
膨進一步上升將是 「無法接受」的，並在10月19日
國會答詢時表示潛在通膨非常接近2%目標。
加以隨著國際油價再度上破110美元大關，短期政策
重點從「對抗通縮 」轉為「防止通膨超標 」，是以
12月再度升息一碼的機率已大幅提高。
另一方，高市內閣的食品消費稅減稅與國防支出增

加，使市場對財源安排存疑 。不過，10月19日高市
在國會承諾在下一財年預算中妥善管控國債發行規
模，使近期長端利率有所回落 。但12月下一財年預
算與國債發行計畫公布時，財政紀律的可信度將再受
檢驗，預期短線利率上行趨緩，維持高檔盤整。
重點摘要
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026/10，中國信託商業銀行整理

不做任何擔保，如有錯漏或疏忽，本行並不負任何法律責任。任何人因信賴此等資料而做出或改變投資決策，應審慎衡量本身之需求及投資之風險，並就投
資結果自行負責。未經本公司許可，本資料及訊息不得逕行抄錄、翻印或另作派發。本行以上市場資訊與分析，不涉及特定投資標的之建議。
《盤勢觀點-資金輪動加速，防禦成長族群接棒》
10月日本股市震盪 、類股表現分化 。市場資金明顯
由上半年漲幅龐大的AI硬體與半導體族群撤出，轉向
軟體服務、醫療保健及內需消費等防禦成長型產業。
其中，東京威力科創、SCREEN、瑞薩電子等指標股
普遍出現雙位數跌幅。相較之下，軟體服務類股大漲
17.9%，BayCurrent、SoftBank等個股表現突出。醫
藥生技、消費零售及娛樂觀光相關族群亦受惠於防禦
需求與內需消費韌性而表現相對亦強勢。值得注意的
是，銀行與非銀行金融股同步走弱，多家大型金融機
構跌幅介於8%至15%，顯示市場擔憂金融條件收緊
可能壓抑經濟活動與資本市場表現。
整體而言，10月日股市場風格由 「AI硬體主導」轉

向「防禦成長與內需消費主導」，同時對經濟基本面
疑慮小幅升溫 ，8~9月相對強勢的金融出現回調壓
力。展望未來，短期盤勢仍受利匯率壓力，以及科技
股評價調整影響 ，加以1H27預估日經225指數企業
獲利年增率轉為負成長，因此本行維持9月以來下調
日股至中性的看法不變，待國際油價回落以及年底年
度預算案底定後，可伺機布局。
重點摘要
5

務、醫療保健及內需消費等防
禦成長族群。半導體、金融與
工業設備股表現疲弱，反映投
資人開始關注高利率環境及企
業獲利成長放緩風險。

不做任何擔保，如有錯漏或疏忽，本行並不負任何法律責任。任何人因信賴此等資料而做出或改變投資決策，應審慎衡量本身之需求及投資之風險，並就投

《盤勢觀點-澳洲景氣放緩，股匯市轉向盤整》
隨著國際油價維持衝高，澳洲央行（RBA）於9月再
度升息將現金利率提高至 4.6%，官員雖表示若通膨
壓力持續，不排除進一步緊縮。但在高利率與高通膨
壓力持續積累下對景氣負面壓力逐漸加大。
資料顯示，澳洲9月失業率升至4.72%，已連續兩個
月 落 在 澳 央 主 席 認 為 可 有 效 壓 抑 通 膨 的 失 業 率
4.5~5.0%區間；而通膨方面 ，9月份澳洲 CPI雖由
4.0%升至4.3%，但核心CPI僅有2.2%，加以住房通
膨明顯回落，因此本行預期澳央轉向觀望、本輪升息
暫時告終，2027年政策轉向觀望。
外需方面，中國經濟基本面持續低迷，消費與投資不
振亦難以挹注澳洲出口動能，外需對澳幣支撐力量有

限，加以預期聯準會在 12月再度升息 ，將呈現「利
差有撐但優勢遞減」格局，因此預估澳幣短線維持在
0.7~0.71區間盤整。
綜上，持續升息後澳洲基本面轉弱，但高油價壓力又
迫使央行貨幣政策難以轉向寬鬆，加以企業獲利未見
改善動能，澳洲股市難以樂觀，本行維持中性展望。
重點摘要
• 高油價迫使澳洲央行維持偏
鷹態度 ，但高利率已逐步壓
抑就業與內需。
• 中國需求偏弱限制出口前景 ，
加上美澳利差優勢收斂 ，預
期澳幣短期維持區間震盪 ，
澳股展望偏中性。

Asia Trends

2026 年10 月 21 日

1/3 
インドネシア中銀、市場動揺も、政策運営は政府と市場の「板挟み」が続く 
～一部の利上げ観測に「ゼロ回答」、様々な政策目標実現へ難しい対応が続くと予想される～ 
 経済調査部 主席エコノミスト  西濵 徹 
（℡：050-5474-7495）

（要旨） 
○ インドネシア中銀は 10 月 20～21 日の理事会で、政策金利のＢＩレートを４会合連続で
5.75％に据え置いた。MSCI が市場の透明性やガバナンスへの懸念を背景に 「スタンドアロー
ン市場」への格下げ案を示し、株安・ルピア安が進んだことで、一部では利上げ観測が浮上
していた。しかし、中銀は５～６月の累計100bp の利上げで物価安定には十分との認識を示
し、預金ファシリティ金利を 25bp 引き上げて 5.00％とするとともに、為替ヘッジ取引への
優遇措置を強化し、通貨安定と海外資金の流入促進を図る判断を示した。 
○ 一方で、 景気下支えや雇用創出に向けて、 預金準備率を11～12 月に段階的に引き下げ、 銀行
の貸出余力を拡大する方針を示した。原油高に伴うインフレ圧力や、米国の金融引き締めに
よるドル高圧力を警戒する一方、国内消費の底堅さや民間投資の持ち直し、外需の回復など
を踏まえ、2026 年の経済成長率見通しは「＋4.9～5.7％」との従来の見通しを据え置いた。 
○ 中銀を巡ってはここ数年、政策目標に物価・通貨の安定に加え、経済成長や雇用創出を支援
する役割が追加されてきたほか、政治介入に対する懸念もある。こうしたなか、先行きも政
府の景気下支え要請と金融市場の通貨安定要求との「板挟み」となり、様々な政策目標の実
現を目指す観点から、一段と難しい政策運営を迫られる状況が続くと予想される。 
【インドネシア中銀、市場の混乱で一部に政策金利の引き上げ観測も、４会合連続で据え置き】 
インドネシア銀行（中銀）は、10 月 20～21 日に開催した月例理事会において、政策金利である
ＢＩレートを４会合連続で 5.75％に据え置くことを決定した。一方で、預金ファシリティ金利を
25bp 引き上げ、5.00％とした。中銀は、５月の月例理事会でルピア相場の安定を目的に50bp の大
幅利上げを決定したものの、その後も金融市場ではルピア安の動きが収まらず、株式、債券も下落
するトリプル安に直面した。このため、中銀は６月９日に開催した週次理事会で 25bp の緊急利上
げを実施するとともに、ルピア相場の安定に向けた追加対策を発表した。さらに、６月の月例理事
会でも 25bp の追加利上げを実施し、約１ヵ月の間に累計 100bp もの利上げを行うなど、通貨安定
に向けて金融引き締めを強化した。その後は、ルピア相場が落ち着きを取り戻したことから、前回
の９月会合まで３会合連続で政策金利を据え置き、様子見姿勢を維持してきた。 
しかし、 足元では同国の金融市場が再び混乱に直面している。 きっかけは、 指数算出会社のMSCI

### Page 2

2/3 
が 10 月15 日、同国市場を「スタンドアローン市場」に格下げする案を示し、市場参加者との協議
を開始したことにある（注１）。MSCI は
１月、同国株式市場の情報開示に懸念を
表明し、透明性向上への取り組みが進ま
なければ、フロンティア市場に格下げす
る可能性を示唆した。これを受けて、当
局は最低浮動株比率の大幅引き上げな
ど、海外投資家の懸念解消に向けた対策
を打ち出し、市場改革を加速させる姿勢
を示した。しかし、その後も市場参加者
からは、協調的な取引行為への疑念が解
消されておらず、実効性のある法執行も
確認できないとの指摘が MSCI に寄せられた。また、フロンティア市場への格下げでは、問題の深
刻さが適切に反映されないとの意見も寄せられた。このため、MSCI は 2027 年４月末まで市場参加
者の意見を募り、 同年６月末までに最終判断を示すとした。 協議を通じて市場のガバナンスや機能
の実質的な改善を裏付ける明確な証拠が確認されない限り、 格下げを進める方針である。 この発表
を受けて、主要株価指数（ジャカルタ総合指数）は一段と下振れしており、ルピア相場も下落して
いる（図１） 。 
こうしたなか、金融市場においては、月例理事会を前に、中銀が政策金利の引き上げを含む市場
安定策を打ち出すとの見方が浮上した。また、中銀は今回、会合後の記者会見の開始時刻を前倒し
すると発表した。中銀では、７月にペリー前総裁が任期を２年弱残して突然辞任し、上級副総裁で
あったデストリ・ダマヤンティ氏が総裁代行を務めた後、９月２日に総裁へ正式就任した。今回の
月例理事会は、デストリ氏の正式就任後２回目の定例会合に当たる。しかし、中銀は今回もＢＩレ
ートの引き上げを見送る一方、 預金ファシリティ金利の引き上げや為替ヘッジ取引への優遇措置の
強化を通じて、市場の安定を図る判断を示した。

【中銀は様々な政策目標の実現に向け、政府と市場の間で「板挟み」となることが予想される】 
会合後に公表した声明文では、 今回の決定について 「イラン情勢の緊迫化や米国の金融引き締め
による対外的な圧力が続くなかでのルピア相場の安定」、「2026 年と 2027 年の物価目標の達成」を
目的とする政策ミックスの一環との考えを示した。一方で、 景気下支えと国内の流動性確保に向け
て、 預金準備率の引き下げを決定した。 一般商業銀行向けは現行の９％から、11 月１日に８％、12
月１日に７％へ、 イスラム銀行向けは現行の7.5％から、11 月１日に6.5％、12 月１日に5.5％へ、
それぞれ段階的に引き下げる。さらに、為替ヘッジ取引への優遇措置を強化し、海外からの資金流

注１ 10 月16 日付レポート「インドネシア株に逆風、MSCI が「スタンドアローン」への格下げ検討」 
  （https://www.dlri.co.jp/report/macro/666610.html）

### Page 3

本資料は情報提供を目的として作成されたものであり、投資勧誘を目的としたものではありません。作成時点で、第一ライフ資産運用
経済研究所が信ずるに足ると判断した情報に基づき作成していますが、その正確性、完全性に対する責任は負いません。見通しは予告
なく変更されることがあります。また、記載された内容は、第一生命保険ないしはその関連会社の投資方針と常に整合的であるとは限
りません。 
3/3 
入を促す方針を示した。 
世界経済については「イラン情勢の悪化による原油価格の高止まりを受けて、 景気の下押しとイ
ンフレ圧力が併存している」との認識を示す一方、 「2026 年の世界経済成長率は＋３％となる」と
の従来見通しを維持した。 また、 「米国で追加利上げの可能性が高まっているうえ、 財政政策を理由
に米国債金利は高止まりしている」 として、 「新興国への資金流入が抑制され、 ドル高圧力が続く可
能性がある」との見通しを示した。 
一方、 同国経済について 「財政支援や消費者心理の改善を追い風に家計消費は底堅く推移してい
る」としつつ、足元では「企業マインドの改善を背景に民間投資に持ち直しの兆しがでている」と
の認識を示した。先行きは「外需の増加
も見込まれる」として、 「2026 年の経済
成長率は＋4.9～5.7％になる」との従来
見通しを据え置いた。そして、対外収支
も「世界的な不確実性の高まりの影響を
緩和すべく、国際収支を強化する必要が
ある」 としつつ、 「９月末時点の外貨準備
高は輸入の5.3 ヵ月分を有する」とし、
「2026 年の経常赤字はＧＤＰ比▲2.4～
▲1.6％」になると３会合ぶりに見通し
を示した。ルピア相場については「為替
介入やヘッジ取引への優遇措置などを通じた安定強化を図っている」 と従来からの対策を強化する
考えを示した。 そして、 物価動向については 「政府が設定した目標の範囲内で維持されている」 （図
２） ほか、 足元では 「天候不順による食料インフレの懸念が高まっている」ものの、「目標域内に収
めるべく、為替相場の安定に加え、政府も食料インフレ対策を強化する」との見方を示した。 
会合後に記者会見に臨んだ中銀のデストリ総裁は、MSCI による発表について 「株式市場の動向を
注視している」との見方を示した。そのうえで、政策運営について「的を絞ったものにしたい」と
して、 「海外からの投資の呼び込みに資するものにしたい」との考えを示した。そして、 「我々は５
月以降すでに100bp の利上げを実施しており、 物価安定には十分」 、 「海外投資家に対してインセン
ティブを提供している」との考えを示した。先行きの政策運営については「データ次第になる」と
して明言を避けた。また、ソリキン副総裁は、預金準備率引き下げの狙いについて「雇用創出を目
的としたもの」 、 「銀行の貸し出し余力の拡大を目指したもの」と説明した。そして、今回の政策対
応について「様々な政策目的に対応することを目指したもの」と説明した。 
こうした発言の背景には、ここ数年、中銀に対して、物価と通貨の安定に加え、経済成長や雇用
創出を支援する役割も求められるようになっていることがあると考えられる。 政治介入が懸念され
る動きもみられるなか、中銀の政策運営はこれまで以上に難しくなっている。先行きも、中銀は政
府からの景気下支え要請と、金融市場からの通貨安定要求との「板挟み」に置かれるとともに、難
しい政策運営を迫られる局面が続くと予想される。                  以 上

生産
7月の鉱工業生産（東海3県）は前月比＋2.2％と2ヵ月ぶりに増加した。6月に電子部品・デバイス、輸送機械
が減少し全体を引き下げたが、7月は電子部品・デバイスが増加に転じた。東海の生産は、全体として水準も
高く、増加基調にあるとみられる。先行きについては高水準での推移を見込むが、米・イランの対立長期化に
より下振れるリスクもある。

企業景況感（日銀短観）
日銀短観9月調査の業況判断DIは、全産業で＋22ptと6月調査(＋19pt)から改善した。製造業では、非鉄金
属、生産用機械、電気機械、自動車等が改善し、全体でも改善となる一方、非製造業では建設、小売、情報
通信などが悪化し、全体としては小幅悪化したものの、引き続き高い水準を維持している。先行き（12月）につ
いては、製造業、非製造業とも悪化が見込まれている。

6 Mitsubishi UFJ Research and Consulting
設備投資（日銀短観）
日銀短観9月調査によると、26年度の設備投資は全産業で前年比＋6.0％と前回6月調査から上方修正と
なった。業種別では、食料品、鉄鋼、電気機械、不動産、物品賃貸、情報通信などが大幅増を計画している。
